.
∎

Corollary 3.24
.

Weak equivalences lift against fibrant objects up to homotopy.
∎

Lemma 3.25
.

Consider a commuting diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X}

where the middle vertical map is a homotopy equivalence between fibrant objects.
Then the composite square has a diagonal filler as indicated.

Proof.

Factor
S\to T
into an anodyne map
S\to M
followed by a fibration
M\to T
.
By 2-out-of-3 for homotopy equivalences between fibrant objects and
corollaries

3.16
and
3.17
, we have that
M\to T
is a trivial fibration.
Then the desired lift is the composite of a lift of the cofibration
A\to B
against the trivial fibration
M\to Y
and a lift of the anodyne map
S\to M
against the fibration
Y\to X
.
∎

The following statement is a relative version the “generalized extension property of Kan
\Delta
-sets“ of
[
RS71
, Corollary 5.4]
, but with our a priori weaker notion of weak equivalence.

Corollary 3.26
.

Cofibrations that are weak equivalences lift against fibrations between fibrant objects.

Proof.

Combine
lemmas

3.23
and
3.25
.
∎

Remark 3.27
.

Any anodyne map is a cofibration and a weak equivalence, but the reverse direction does not hold.
The smallest counterexample is the
Dunce hat
inclusion
\Delta^{0}\to D
where
D
is the semisimplicial set with one vertex, one edge, and one triangle.
Taking the pushout

\textstyle{\Delta^{0}\star L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}\star L}
\textstyle{\Delta^{1}\star L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces Q\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

the horizontal maps are anodyne, as can be checked for the composite

\textstyle{\Delta^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

showing by 2-out-of-3 that the cofibration
\Delta^{0}\to D
is a weak equivalence.
If
\Delta^{0}\to D
was anodyne, it would arise as a codomain retract of an
\omega
-composition
\Delta^{0}\to S
of pushouts of coproducts of horn inclusions, but
S
cannot contain a triangle with identical sides as any horn filling comes with a “fresh” side.
∎

Given a category
\mathcal{D}
, the geometric monoidal structure and the interval lift to
[\mathcal{D},\widehat{\Delta_{+}}]
, inducing a notion of homotopy there.

Lemma 3.28
.

Homotopies are closed under limit and colimit, i.e. the limit and colimit functors from
[\mathcal{D},\widehat{\Delta_{+}}]
to
\widehat{\Delta_{+}}
preserve the homotopy relation.

Proof.

For the limit functor this is direct, for the colimit functor use the path object representation (i.e. a homotopy between maps
A\to B
is a map
A\to[I,B]
).
∎

Corollary 3.29
.

Homotopies are closed under product and coproduct (of arbitrary arity), i.e. if
f_{i}\sim g_{i}
for all
i\in I
, then
\prod_{i\in I}f_{i}\sim\prod_{i\in I}g_{i}
and
\coprod_{i\in I}f_{i}\sim\coprod_{i\in I}g_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 3.30
.

Homotopy equivalences are closed under product and coproduct (of arbitrary arity).
∎

Lemma 3.31
.

The functors
-\otimes-
and
\hom(-,-)
preserve homotopies in each arguments.

Proof.

By formal properties of the geometric symmetric monoidal structure.
∎

Corollary 3.32
.

The functors
-\otimes-
and
\hom(-,-)
preserve homotopy equivalences in each arguments.
∎

Corollary 3.33
.

For
X
fibrant, the functor
\hom(-,X)
preserves weak equivalences.

Proof.

Note first that
\hom(j,X)
is a trivial fibration between fibrant objects for
j
anodyne.
The claim then follows from this and
corollary

3.32
by inspecting the definition of weak equivalence and 2-out-of-3.
∎

Corollary 3.34
.

For
X
fibrant, the functor
\hom(-,X)
sends cofibrations that are weak equivalences to trivial fibrations.

Lemma 3.35
.

The following are equivalent for a map
f
:

(i)

f
is a weak equivalence,

(ii)

\hom(f,X)
is a homotopy equivalence for all fibrant
X
,

(iii)

\tau_{0}(\hom(f,X))
is a bijection all fibrant
X
, i.e. precomposition with
f
induces a bijection of homotopy classses when the target is fibrant.

Proof.

The direction from (i) to (ii) is
corollary

3.33
.
If (ii) holds, then clearly does (iii).
For the direction from (iii) to (i), we may reduce to the case that
A
and
B
are fibrant by unfolding the definition of weak equivalence and using 2-out-of-3 for bijections.
Then
f
is a weak equivalence exactly if it becomes an isomorphism in the homotopy category of
\widehat{\Delta_{+}}
.
Noting that a map in an isomorphism exactly if precomposition with it induces bijections, this corresponds exactly to the given condition.
∎

Corollary 3.36
.

A cofibration
m
is a weak equivalence exactly if the fibration
\hom(m,Z)
is trivial for all fibrant
Z
.
∎

Corollary 3.37
.

Anodyne maps are weak equivalences.

The following statement is useful when reducing certain statements about weak equivalences to anodyne maps.

Corollary 3.38
.

Weak equivalences are the closure of anodyne maps, more specifically just
\omega
-compositions of pushouts of coproducts of horn inclusions, under 2-out-of-6.

Proof.

By
corollaries

3.37
and
3.22
.
Note that closure under codomain retracts follows from closure under 2-out-of-6.
∎

The significance of the following (standard) corollary is in its applications to homotopy equivalences between non-fibrant objects.

Corollary 3.39
.

Homotopy equivalences are weak equivalences.
∎

Corollary 3.40
.

Weak equivalences are closed under coproduct (of arbitrary arity).

Proof.

We use the characterization of weak equivalences given by part (ii) of
lemma

3.35
.
Then the claim reduces to closure of homotopy equivalences between fibrant objects under product, given by
corollary

3.30
.
∎

Corollary 3.41
.

Weak equivalences are closed under retract.

Proof.

We use the characterization of weak equivalences given by part (ii) of
lemma

3.35
.
Then the claim reduces to closure of homotopy equivalences under retracts.
∎

Lemma 3.42
.

Weak equivalences that are cofibrations are closed under pushout as well as
\omega
-composition.

Proof.

Recall that we have corresponding closure statements for cofibrations.
Under
corollary

3.36
, the claims then reduce to the dual closure statements of trivial fibrations, namely pushouts in spans of fibrant objects and
\omega^{\operatorname{op}}
-compositions.
∎

Theorem 3.43
.

Semisimplicial sets form a cofibration category.

Proof.

The only non-trivial conditions of a cofibration category here are items
(3)
and
(5)
of the definition, which are given by
lemma

3.42
and
corollary

3.22
, respectively.
∎

Question 3.44
.

Are cofibrations that are weak equivalences the left class of a weak factorization system?
∎

Lemma 3.45
.

The geometric product preserves weak equivalences, i.e. if
u
and
v
are weak equivalences, then so is
u\otimes v
.

Proof.

By 2-out-of-3, we can reduce to the case where
v=\operatorname{id}_{A}
for some
A
.
We use the characterization of weak equivalences given by part (ii) of
lemma

3.35
.
Then the claim reduces to closure of homotopy equivalences between fibrant objects under
\hom(A,-)
, which holds by
corollary

3.32
.
∎

Note that the cartesian product does not preserve weak equivalences: the inclusion
\delta^{0}\colon\Delta^{0}\to\Delta^{1}
is a weak equivalence, but
\top\times\delta^{0}\colon\Delta^{0}\to\Delta^{0}+\Delta^{0}
is not.

Corollary 3.46
.

If
u
is a weak equivalence and cofibration and
v
is any map, then the Leibniz geometric product
u\mathbin{\widehat{\otimes}}v
is a weak equivalence.
In particular, cofibrations that are weak equivalences are closed under Leibniz geometric product with cofibrations.

Proof.

The first part is
lemmas

3.45
and
3.42
using 2-out-of-3.
The second part follows with
lemma

3.4
.
∎

Lemma 3.47
.

There is map of cylinders

\textstyle{I\otimes(A\star B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\star B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{0}\otimes(A\star B)}
\scriptstyle{(\delta_{0}\otimes A)\star(\delta_{0}\otimes B)}
\textstyle{A\star B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}\otimes(A\star B)}
\scriptstyle{(\delta_{1}\otimes A)\star(\delta_{1}\otimes B)}
\textstyle{(I\otimes A)\star(I\otimes B)}

natural in semisimplicial sets
A
and
B
.

Proof.

Let us extend the endofunctor
I\otimes-
from semisimplicial sets to augmented semisimplicial sets using left Kan extension (note that it restricts back to the original on semisimplicial sets by fully faithfulness of the embedding).
We will establish the map of cylinders in augmented semisimplicial sets.
Here, both cylinder functors are separately cocontinuous in
A
and
B
.
It will thus suffice to produce the map on the restriction to representables.

Let
A=\Delta^{a}
and
B=\Delta^{b}
with
a,b\geq-1
.
An
n
-simplex of
I\otimes(\Delta^{a}\star\Delta^{b})
is maps
[n]\to[1]
and
[n]\to[a]\star[b]
in categories that are jointly monic.
The second map induces a decomposition
[n]=[a^{\prime}]\star[b^{\prime}]
and maps
[a^{\prime}]\to[a]
and
[b^{\prime}]\to[b]
.
By cancellation of monomorphisms, we have that
[a^{\prime}]\to[n]\to[1]
and
[a^{\prime}]\to[a]
as well as
[b^{\prime}]\to[b]\to[1]
and
[b^{\prime}]\to[b]
are jointly monic.
This is an
a^{\prime}
-simplex in
I\otimes\Delta^{a}
and an
b^{\prime}
-simplex in
I\otimes\Delta^{b}
, together an
n
-simplex in
(I\otimes\Delta^{a})\star(I\otimes\Delta^{b})
.
One may check this operation is natural in
[a],[b]\in\Delta_{+,{\mathsf{aug}}}
.
It also clearly commutes with the cylinder endpoint inclusions.
∎

Question 3.48
.

The decomposition of
n
in the above proof essentially uses parametric right adjointness of the join.
One may wonder whether this can be turned into a more abstract argument.
∎

Corollary 3.49
.

The join monoidal structure preserves homotopies and homotopy equivalences, i.e. given homotopies
f_{0}\sim f_{1}
and
g_{0}\sim g_{1}
, we have
f_{0}\star g_{0}\sim f_{1}\star g_{1}
, and given homotopy equivalences
u
and
v
, then
u\star v
is a homotopy equivalence.
∎

Note that the nullary case is also included in the preceding statement, but is not interesting.

Corollary 3.50
.

The join monoidal structure preserves weak equivalences, i.e. given weak equivalences
u
and
v
, then
u\star v
is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Note that a map
A\to B
is a weak equivalence precisely if there is a square

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{anod}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{anod}}
\textstyle{Y}

with
X\to Y
a homotopy equivalence (not necessarily between fibrant objects); in one direction this is by definition, in the other by
corollaries

3.39
and
3.37
and 2-out-of-3.
The join monoidal structure preserves anodyne maps by
lemma

3.9
and homotopy equivalences by
corollary

3.49
, thus also preserves weak equivalences.
∎

Corollary 3.51
.

The Leibniz join of a cofibration that is a weak equivalence with any map is a weak equivalence.
Cofibrations that are weak equivalences are closed under Leibniz join with cofibrations.
∎

The following statement and its proof are essentially
[
Sch13
, Proposition 2.6]
.
It is a finitary version of
corollary

3.38
.

Lemma 3.52
.

In the full subcategory of finite semisimplicial sets, the class of weak equivalences is the closure under 2-out-of-6 of pushouts of horn inclusions.

Proof.

Let
W^{\prime}
denote the closure of the statement.
It will suffice to show that every weak equivalence
f\colon A\to B
admits a composable weak equivalence
g
such that
gf\in W^{\prime}
.
The claim then follows by applying this statement again to
g
and using 2-out-of-6.

Let
A_{0}=_{\operatorname{def}}A
and
A_{n+1}
be obtained by filling all (finitely many) possible horns in
A_{n}
that have not already been filled in a previous step.
Then every inclusion in the sequence

\textstyle{A_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots}

is a finite composition of pushouts of horn inclusion and the colimit
X
is fibrant.
Using
corollary

3.24
, we may solve the lifting problem

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

up to homotopy.
Since
B+_{A}I\otimes A
is finite, there is
n
such that this lift and the associated homotopy lift through
A_{n}\to X
, yielding a commuting diagram

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{0}\otimes A}
\textstyle{I\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{n}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{1}\otimes A}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}

All solid maps here are weak equivalences, hence so is
g
by 2-out-of-3.
Furthermore, the left vertical maps and the top right map lie in
W^{\prime}
, hence so does
gf
by 2-out-of-3.
∎

Question 3.53
.

One may wonder if the preceding statement can be strengthed.

•

Is
lemma

3.52
still true with 2-out-of-6 replaced by 2-out-of-3?

•

Given a cofibration
A\to B
between finite simplicial sets that is a weak equivalence, is there
B\to C
such that both
B\to C
and the composite
A\to C
are finite composites of pushouts of horn inclusions?

∎

3.7.
Comparison with simplicial sets

Write
\Delta_{-}
for the wide subcategory of
\Delta
consisting of epimorphisms.
Then
(\Delta_{-},\Delta_{+})
forms a Reedy structure on
\Delta
.
Let us write
U
for the forgetful functor from simplicial sets to semisimplicial sets, induced by restriction along the inclusion
i\colon\Delta_{+}\to\Delta
.
Left and right Kan extensions along
i
induce adjunctions
L\dashv U\dashv R
where
L
and
R
denote the free and cofree simplicial set functor, respectively.
We will be particularly interested in the induced monad
(UL,\eta,\mu)
.

The
interval
in simplicial sets is given by the image of the semisimplicial inverval under
L
and will be denoted using the same symbols.
Note that it has a contraction and connections.
With the cartesian monoidal structure, this induces associated notions of cylinder, homotopy, homotopy equivalence.
Recall that
L
sends the geometric to the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure, so that
L
functorially preserves homotopies and homotopy equivalences.

By adjointness, we obtain the following statement.

Lemma 3.54
.

Functorially in a simplicial set
A
, there is cylinder morphism from the semisimplicial cylinder on
UA
to the image under
U
of the simplicial cylinder on
A
.

Proof.

By adjointness and monoidality of
L
, a cylinder map

(I\otimes UA,\delta_{0}\otimes UA,\delta_{1}\otimes UA)\to U(I\times A,\delta_{0}\times A,\delta_{1}\times A)

corresponds to a cylinder map

(I\times LUA,\delta_{0}\times LUA,\delta_{1}\times LUA)\to(I\times A,\delta_{0}\times A,\delta_{1}\times A).

This is given by the counit of the adjunction
L\dashv U
.
∎

Corollary 3.55
.

The forgetful functor
U
functorially preserves homotopies and homotopy equivalences.
∎

Let
S
be the subcategory of
[[1],\Delta]
with objects restricted to maps in
\Delta_{-}
and codomain part of morphisms restricted to maps in
\Delta_{+}
.
The evident fully faithful functor
S\to\Delta\downarrow i
gives rise to a morphism of two-sided opfibrations (in particular the left legs are Grothendieck fibrations and the right legs are Grothendieck opfibrations) as below:

(3.5)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.16667pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-39.96034pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern i\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.15959pt\raise-34.96332pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.16669pt\raise-23.10881pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.36047pt\raise-34.96332pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.5006pt\raise-24.26358pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Note that
\operatorname{ev}_{0}
is a discrete Grothendieck fibration.
The map
S\to\Delta\downarrow i
is a coreflective embedding: its right adjoint sends a map in
\Delta
to the left factor of its Reedy factorization.
This restricts to coreflective embeddings of fibers over
\Delta
.

The functor
L
is given by restriction along
\pi_{1}^{\operatorname{op}}
followed by left Kan extension along
\pi_{0}^{\operatorname{op}}
, which is computed as fiberwise colimits since
\pi_{0}^{\operatorname{op}}
is a Grothendieck opfibration.
Since
S\to\Delta\downarrow i
has fiberwise (over
\Delta
) right adjoints, its opposite is fiberwise final.
Thus, the left Kan extension along
\pi_{0}^{\operatorname{op}}
is also given by restriction along (the opposite of)
S\to(\Delta\downarrow i)
followed by fiberwise coproducts along
\operatorname{ev}_{0}^{\operatorname{op}}
(since
\operatorname{ev}_{0}^{\operatorname{op}}
is a discrete Grothendieck opfibration).
In summary, we can describe
L
as restriction along
\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{\operatorname{op}}
followed by fiberwise coproducts along
\operatorname{ev}_{0}^{\operatorname{op}}
.
Explicitly, given
X\in\widehat{\Delta_{+}}
and
[n]\in\Delta
, the set
LX_{n}
consists of pairs
(s,x)
where
s\colon[n]\to[k]
is in
\Delta_{-}
and
x\in X_{k}
.
The action of a map
f\colon[m]\to[n]
in
\Delta
on
(s,x)
is
(s^{\prime},x[d^{\prime}])
where
(s^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is the Reedy factorization of the map
sf
in
\Delta
.

Let us think of presheaves over a category
\mathcal{C}
as discrete Grothendieck fibrations over
\mathcal{C}
.
We generalize the notion of linear polynomial functors in tbe wide subcategory of
\mathbf{Cat}
on discrete Grothendieck fibrations by allowing the right leg to be any functor, not just a discrete Grothendieck fibration.
Then
L
is the linear polynomial functor specified by the top span in (
3.5
), i.e. pullback along
\operatorname{ev}_{1}
and postcomposition with
\operatorname{ev}_{0}
.

Given a category
A
, let us write
[A,\Delta]_{-}^{+}
for the subcategory of
[A,\Delta]
with objects restricted to functors
A\to\Delta_{-}
and morphisms restricted to natural transformations with components in
\Delta_{+}
.
These categories will play an important role in the relationship between semisimplicial and simplicial sets.
Note that we have a pullback

\textstyle{[[1],\Delta]_{-}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\Delta\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

In particular, as for
S
, the evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{0}\colon[[1],\Delta]_{-}^{+}\to\Delta_{+}
is a discrete Grothendieck fibration.
The composite linear polynomial functor
UL
is specified by the span

\textstyle{[[1],\Delta]_{-}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{1}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{+}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{+}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Note that the
n
-th power of
UL
is the linear polynomial functor specified by the span

\textstyle{[[n],\Delta]_{-}^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{n}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{+}}
\textstyle{\Delta_{+}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

The unit and multiplication of the monad
UL
are given by maps of linear polynomial functors

So
UL
is a linear polynomial monad (in our generalized sense), in particular a cartesian monad.

Lemma 3.56
.

The natural transformations
\eta UL
and
UL\eta
are homotopic in
[\widehat{\Delta_{+}},\widehat{\Delta_{+}}]
.

Proof.

Using the cylinder map of
lemma

3.54
(with
L(-)
for
A
), it will suffice to construct a homotopy
H
with respect to the cylinder on
UL
given by the image under
U
of the simplicial cylinder on
L
:

\textstyle{UL\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{UL\delta_{0}\times UL}
\scriptstyle{UL\eta}
\textstyle{ULI\times UL\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{ULUL\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\textstyle{UL\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{UL\delta\times UL}
\scriptstyle{\eta UL}

Note that the diagram lives over
UL
, with the indexing of
ULUL
given by the monad multiplication.

Switching to the viewpoint of linear polynomial functors, the problem then becomes to find a map fitting as indicated into the commuting diagram

As maps between summits of (generalized) linear functors, (
3.18
) is a diagram of discrete Grothendieck fibrations over
[[1],\Delta]_{-}^{+}
.
It will thus suffice to define the action of
H
on objects and morphisms compatible with the indexing; preservation of identities and compositions will hold for free.
Similarly, it will suffice to check commutativity of the upper and lower triangles on objects; commutativity on morphisms will hold for free.

is a span in

as below:

is given by dotted maps commuting as indicated.
The behaviour of
H
on an object (
3.19
) should be to functorially construct a factorization of
[n]\to[k]
in
\Delta_{-}
whose left and right factors are trivial if
[n]\to[1]
is constantly
0
and
1
, respectively.

Let us construct the action of
H
on the object (
3.19
).
Let
[n_{1}]\to[n]
be the pullback in
\Delta_{\mathsf{aug}}
of
1\colon[0]\to[1]
along
[n]\to[1]
.
We construct the Reedy factorization (dashed arrows) and take the pushout drawn below:

\textstyle{[n_{1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k_{1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[m]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k]\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

We define
[n]\to[m]\to[k]
as the result of
H
.
We check the boundary conditions:

•

If
[n]\to[1]
is constantly
0
, then
[a]
is initial.
Then
[a]\to[k]
is mono, so
[a]\to[b]
is iso, and hence is
[n]\to[m]
.

•

If
[n]\to[1]
is constantly
1
, then
[a]\to[n]
is iso.
Then
[b]\to[k]
and
[b]\to[m]
are iso, and hence is
[m]\to[k]
.

Let us construct the action of
H
on the morphism (
3.20
).
Let
[n^{\prime}]\to[m^{\prime}]\to[k^{\prime}]
be the value of
H
on the codomain, constructed from a pullback
[n_{1}^{\prime}]\to[n^{\prime}]
of
1\colon[0]\to[1]
, a Reedy factorization
[n_{1}^{\prime}]\to[k_{1}^{\prime}]\to[k^{\prime}]
, and a pushout as before.
We obtain a commuting diagram

\textstyle{[n_{1}^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{[n_{1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[n]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k_{1}^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{[m^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[k_{1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[m]}

where the front and back squares are pushouts and the top square is a pullback by pullback pasting.
The pushouts induce a map
[m^{\prime}]\to[m]
, which is in

by

The desired result of

is depicted below:

\textstyle{[n^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{[k]\hbox to0.0pt{;\hss}}

note that the right square commutes because
[n^{\prime}]\to[m^{\prime}]
is epi.
∎

Lemma 3.57
.

In
\Delta_{\mathsf{aug}}
, consider a diagram

\textstyle{A^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}

where the vertical maps are in
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},-}
and the horizontal maps except for
Y^{\prime}\to Y
are in
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},-}
.
Assume that the front square is a pushout and the top square is a pullback.
Then the back square is a pushout exactly if the bottom right map
Y^{\prime}\to Y
is in
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},+}
.

Proof.

Assume that the forward direction holds.
Then the reverse direction can be derived as follows.
We introduce the pushout
Y^{\prime}_{0}
in the back square (recall that
\Delta_{\mathsf{aug}}
has pushouts along maps in
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},+}
, that these pushouts preserve maps in
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},-}
, and that
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},+}
is closed under pushout).
Thus, we obtain another cubical diagram with
Y^{\prime}
replaced by
Y^{\prime}_{0}
.
By assumption, the induced map
Y^{\prime}_{0}\to Y
is in
\Delta_{{\mathsf{aug}},+}
.
By cancellation, so is the induced map
Y^{\prime}_{0}\to Y^{\prime}
.
Again by cancellation, since
B^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
is in
\Delta_{-}
, so is
Y^{\prime}_{0}\to Y^{\prime}
.
Thus,
Y^{\prime}_{0}\to Y^{\prime}
is iso, making the back square a pushout.

It will thus suffice to show the forward direction.
Note that the forgetful functor to finite sets creates epis and monos and preserves the pullbacks and pushouts in the diagram.
Thus, it is enough to verify the corresponding assertion for finite sets.
Let us do this now.

Let
y^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{1}\in Y^{\prime}
have the same image
y\in Y
.
Note that
Y^{\prime}
can be descibed as the coproduct of
X^{\prime}
with the complement of the image of
A^{\prime}
in
B^{\prime}
(using that finite sets are Boolean), and similarly for the front pushout.
We perform case distinction on which component
y^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{1}
lie in.

•

Assume there are respective preimages
x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1}\in X^{\prime}
.
Since
X^{\prime}\to Y
is mono, we have
x^{\prime}_{0}=x^{\prime}_{1}
, hence
y^{\prime}_{0}=y^{\prime}_{1}
.

•

Assume they have respective preimages
b^{\prime}_{0},b^{\prime}_{1}\in B^{\prime}
that do not lie in the image of
A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
.
Because the top square is a pullback, the induced elements
b_{0},b_{1}\in B
do not lie in the image of
A\to B
.
Since both map to
y\in Y
, we have
b_{0}=b_{1}
.
Since
B^{\prime}\to B
is mono, we then have
b^{\prime}_{0}=b^{\prime}_{1}
, hence
y^{\prime}_{0}=y^{\prime}_{1}
.

•

Assume
y^{\prime}_{0}
has a preimage
x^{\prime}
in
X^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}_{1}
has a preimage
b^{\prime}
in
B^{\prime}
(the dual case is analogous).
As a van Kampen pushout, the front square is also a pullback.
By pullback pasting, the composite square from
A^{\prime}\to B^{\prime}
to
X\to Y
is a pullback.
Let
x\in X
be the image of
x^{\prime}
.
By assumption,
b^{\prime}
and
x
map to the same element
y\in Y
.
Thus, they have a common preimage
a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
.
By construction,
a^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}
have the same image in
X
.
Since
X^{\prime}\to X
is mono,
x^{\prime}
is the image of
a^{\prime}
.
Since
x^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
have a common preimage in
A^{\prime}
, they map to the same element in
Y^{\prime}
, i.e.
y^{\prime}_{0}=y^{\prime}_{1}
.
∎

Question 3.58
.

It would be nice to have an abstract categorical argument replacing the last paragraph of the above proof that works in any suitably exact category without the assumption that it is Boolean.
∎

3.8.
Comparison with Kan complexes

Let us define weak factorization systems (cofibration, trivial fibration) and (trivial cofibration, fibration) in simplicial sets generated by the images of boundary inclusions and horn inclusions under
L
, respectively.
These are exactly the usual boundary and horn inclusions in simplicial sets.
By definition,
U
creates fibrations and creates trivial fibrations from maps lifting against cofibrations.
By cocontinuity,
L
preserves cofibrations and sends anodyne maps to trivial cofibrations.

The cofibrations in simplicial sets are the monomorphisms
A\to B
for which the relative latching object inclusions are decidable.
In particular, the cofibrant objects are those for which it is decidable if a simplex is degenerate.
By induction on dimension, one sees that a cofibration in simplicial sets is in particular levelwise decidable.
It follows that
U
preserves cofibrations.

Remark 3.59
.

Let
X
be fibrant and have a vertex
a
.
One might hope that
LX
is fibrant.
However, this is not the case.
Consider the
\Lambda^{2}_{2}
-horn in
LX
that on
d_{1}
is
(s_{0},a)
and on
d_{2}
is
(\operatorname{id}_{[1]},l)
for a loop
l
on
a
given by fibrancy.
Assume that this horn has a filler
(t,y)
.
Let
td_{1}=ut^{\prime}
for uniquely determined face map
u
and degeneracy map
t^{\prime}
; then
(s_{0},a)=(t,y)[d_{1}]=(t^{\prime},y[u])
.
Since
t^{\prime}
is constant and
d_{1}
preserves terminal and initial objects, also
t
is constant, i.e.
t\colon[2]\to[0]
.
This gives a contradiction
(\operatorname{id}_{[1]},l)=(t,y)[d_{2}]=(s_{0},y)
.

One might think that this sort of problem only appears for outer horns.
Unfortunately, it is also not generally the case that
LX
lifts against inner horn inclusions if
X
does.
Lifts for
LX
against horn inclusions of dimension up to three exist.
However, there is another problem in dimension four.
Consider a
\Lambda^{4}_{3}
-horn in
LX
that on
d_{0}
is an
s_{0}
-degeneracy, on
d_{4}
is an
s_{1}
-degeneracy, and on
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
is different tetrahedra (
X
can be freely generated to admit this data).
Then any filler would be an
s_{1}
-degeneracy and have identical sides on
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
, a contradiction.
∎

Constructively, the problem with simplicial sets over semisimplicial sets is that not every simplicial set is cofibrant, only those for which we can decide whether a given element is degenerate.
The property that every object is cofibrant permeates the theory of Kan complexes (i.e. fibrant objects).
For example, by formal properties one has that
X^{A}
is fibrant if
A
is cofibrant and
X
is fbrant.
One would like this to be valid for general
A
, but removing the cofibrancy assumption results in a statement that is not valid constructively
[
BC15
,
BCP15
]
(note that the arguably more primitive problem that there can be non-cofibrant objects, although not discussed in the cited articles, can be observed in an even simpler Kripke model).
Restricting attenting to cofibrant objects is also insufficient as for example the cotensor of a cofibrant object with a countably infinite set is not in general cofibrant.

Using classical logic, one may prove that the two weak factorization systems in simplicial sets as above form a model structure.
In fact, the only classical statement needed is precisely that every object is cofibrant; this is the development in
[
Sat17
]
.
One may then ask how the forgetful functor and its adjoints relate the homotopy theories of simplicial and semisimplicial sets.
Previous developments of the homotopy theory of semisimplicial sets have usually defined weak equivalences as being created by the free simplicial set functor (or equivalently the realization to CW-complexes).
Since we took a more synthetic approach, letting weak equivalences essentially be generated by semisimplicial horn inclusions, we have an a priori weaker notion of weak equivalence.
It thus remains to show that
L
reflects weak equivalences.
We will prove this classically in a way that enables us to extract a few useful constructive facts in semisimplicial sets along the way.
For differentiation, those statements that depend on classical logic will be marked as such.

Recall that
\operatorname{Sk}^{n}(1)
is the unique (up to isomorphism) semisimplicial set consisting of just one simplex in each dimension up to
n\geq 0
.
We write
t^{k,n}
for the unique map
1^{(k)}\to 1^{(n)}
for
-1\leq k\leq n
.
This is a cofibration: there is a unique cocartesian morphism (pushout square) from
t^{n-1,n}
to
i^{n}
for
n\geq 0
.

Lemma 3.60
.

There is a unique and cocartesian morphism from the Leibniz join of
t^{a-1,a}
and
t^{b-1,b}
to
t^{a+b,a+b+1}
for
a,b\geq 0
.

Proof.

Uniqueness holds since source and target of
t^{a+b,a+b+1}
are subterminal.

For existence, we have isomorphisms and cocartesian morphisms indicated by solid arrows below:

\textstyle{i^{a}\mathbin{\widehat{\star}}i^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{cocart}}}
\textstyle{i^{a+b+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{cocart}}}
\textstyle{t^{a-1,a}\mathbin{\widehat{\star}}t^{b-1,b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathsf{cocart}}}
\textstyle{t^{a+b,a+b+1}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

By cocartesianness, to find a cocartesian arrow commuting as indicated is to find that arrow after applying the domain functor to the whole diagram.
Then the diagram lives in
n
-truncated semisimplicial sets and the bottom right object is terminal.
∎

Corollary 3.61
.

There is a unique and cocartesian morphism
i^{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\star}}t^{n-1,n}\to t^{n,n+1}
for
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

We have
t^{-1,0}\simeq i^{0}
.
∎

The following statement is false for odd
n
.

Lemma 3.62
.

The map
\operatorname{Sk}^{n}(1)\to\operatorname{Sk}^{n+2}(1)
is a weak equivalence for even
n
.

Proof.

This is by induction on
n
.
The base case

is given by

, although it can also be seen as an instance of the induction step once

.
Just for this proof, we will abbreviate

is a weak equivalence.

Figure 1.
Colimiting cubes for
X_{0}
to

used in the proof of
lemma

3.62

Now consider the colimits in
fig.

1
, each over a diagram of shape
S(\{0,1,2\})^{\operatorname{op}}
where
S(T)
is the category of non-empty subobjects of a finite set
T
(note that
S([n])\simeq\Delta_{+}/[n]
).
Any map
T_{1}\to T_{2}
of finite sets induces a Grothendieck fibration
S(T_{1})\to S(T_{2})
given by postcomposition followed by taking the image; this operation forms a functor
\mathbf{FinSet}\to\mathbf{Cat}
.
Colimits over
S(\{0,1,2\})^{\operatorname{op}}
, visualized as a cube, reduce to pushouts in three different ways, each corresponding to the choice of an axis
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, by first taking pushouts in the squares orthogonal to this axis and then taking a final pushout of the induced map between pushouts with an induced pushout corner map; this reduction is itself functorial and justified by pasting of left Kan extensions along

\textstyle{S(\{0,1,2\})^{\operatorname{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S(\{0,1,2\}/{\sim})^{\operatorname{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S(\{i\})^{\operatorname{op}}}

where
i\sim i
and
j\sim k
if
j,k\neq i
since left Kan extensions along Grothendieck opfibrations compute as fiberwise colimits.
This reduction to pushouts will be made use of frequently in the following.
In particular, a cocone under a diagram over
S(\{0,1,2\})^{\operatorname{op}}
is colimiting exactly if the induced square between pushout corner maps in the faces parallel to the axis
i
is a pushout.

There are canonical maps between the diagrams in
fig.

1
that by functoriality of colimits give rise to a sequence

(3.21)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.77777pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.77777pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.33331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.33331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X_{4}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Reducing the colimit diagrams for
X_{0}
and
X_{4}
to pushouts with respect to the axis from top to bottom, the pushout corner maps in the top faces are isomorphisms and the bottom faces coincide with the pushout squares defining
A
and
B
.
So we see that the map
X_{0}\to X_{4}
above coincides (up to isomorphism) with the map
A\to B
constructed earlier.
It will thus suffice to show that each step
X_{i}\to X_{i+1}
for
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
is a weak equivalence.

For the first step, we apply the reduction to pushouts with respect to the axis from back to front.
Since the back faces in the colimit diagrams for
X_{0}
and
X_{1}
are identical, by pushout pasting the map
X_{0}\to X_{1}
writes as a pushout of the induced map between the pushouts in the front faces:

\textstyle{1^{(n)}+_{1^{(n-2)}}\Delta^{0}\star 1^{(n-2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\star}}t^{n-2,n}}
\textstyle{X_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{0}\star 1^{(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Thus,
X_{0}\to X_{1}
is a weak equivalence by induction hypothesis,
corollary

3.51
, and
lemma

3.42
.

For the second step, we also use the reduction to pushouts with respect to the axis from back to front.
Again the back faces in the colimit diagrams for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are identical, and the left maps of the front faces are identities.
By pushout pasting, it follows that
X_{1}\to X_{2}
writes as a pushout of the map between the remaining objects:

\textstyle{\Delta^{0}\star 1^{(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{0}\star t^{n,n+1}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{0}\star 1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Hence,
X_{1}\to X_{2}
is a weak equivalence by
lemmas

3.10
and
3.42
(in fact, the left map in the above diagram is just a pushout of
h^{n+2}_{0}
).

For the third step, we yet again use the reduction to pushouts with respect to the axis from back to front.
This time, the spans in the front faces in the colimit diagrams for
X_{2}
and
X_{3}
are identical.
By pushout pasting, it follows that
X_{2}\to X_{3}
writes as a pushout of the pushout corner map in the induced square between the pushout corner maps in the back faces.
Since the join with fixed second argument preserves pushouts, the pushout corner maps in the back faces are just
i^{1}\star 1^{(n-2)}
and
i^{1}\star 1^{(n)}
, respectively.
Hence,
X_{2}\to X_{3}
writes as a pushout of a Leibniz join:

\textstyle{\partial\Delta^{1}\star 1^{(n)}+_{\partial\Delta^{1}\star 1^{(n-2)}}\Delta^{1}\star 1^{(n-2)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{1}\mathbin{\widehat{\star}}t^{n-2,n}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta_{1}\star 1^{(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{3}\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Thus,
X_{2}\to X_{3}
is a weak equivalence by induction hypothesis,
corollary

3.51
, and
lemma

3.42
.

For the fourth step, we use the reduction to pushouts with respect to the axis from left to right.
We first claim that the induced square between pushout corner maps in the left faces in the colimit diagrams for
X_{3}
and
X_{4}
is a pushout.
This means that the cube between the left faces, depicted below, is a colimit diagram:

\textstyle{1^{(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{b\}\star 1^{(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\{b\}\star 1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1^{(n+2)}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Using that same equivalence in a different direction, this means that the induced square between pushout corner maps in the back and front faces of the above cube is a pushout.
But that is precisely the cocartesian morphism of
corollary

3.61
.
Having dealt with that claim, it follows by pushout pasting that
X_{3}\to X_{4}
writes as a pushout of the induced map between the pushouts in the right faces:

\textstyle{1^{(n+1)}+_{1^{(n)}}\Delta^{0}\star 1^{(n)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{1}_{0}\mathbin{\widehat{\star}}t^{n,n+1}}
\textstyle{X_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{1}\star 1^{(n+1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{4}\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Therefore,
X_{3}\to X_{4}
is a weak equivalence by
lemmas

3.9
and
3.42
(in fact, the left map in the above diagram is just a pushout of
h^{n+3}_{0}
).
∎

Note that the preceding proof, while staying at an abstract level of chasing pushouts through diagrams, is completely formal in the following sense: every detail of the proof is specified, there is no hand-waving that obscures hidden laborous details such as manually having to check that two abstractly specified arrows indeed coincide or separately needed manual verification of functoriality or naturality of certain constructions.
One would like every combinatorial proof here to have this level of formality without needing to go down to the level of set-based reasoning.

Remark 3.63
.

All colimits considered in the proof of
lemma

3.62
are actually homotopy colimits as they decompose into pushouts along cofibrations.
As such, instances of the glueing lemma (weakly equivalent spans with one leg a cofibration have weakly equivalent pushouts) in the cofibration category of semisimplicial sets might be used to substitute certain parts of the argument.
It would be nice if it could be used to actually simplify the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.64
.

The map
\top\to 1
is a cofibration and a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Note that
\top\to 1
arises as the
\omega
-composite

\textstyle{\operatorname{Sk}^{0}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sk}^{2}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ldots\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Thus, the claim follows from
lemma

3.62
.
∎

Lemma 3.65
.

The unit
\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}\to UL
is valued in cofibrations that are weak equivalences.

Proof.

Viewing
\eta
as a map of linear polynomial functors, note that the map between the span summits in (
3.7
) is a decidable inclusion.
It follows that
\eta
is valued in cofibrations.
Since
UL
preserves cofibrations, it follows that Leibniz application of
\eta
preserves cofibrations.

It remains to prove that
\eta
is valued in weak equivalences.
We generalize to Leibniz application of
\eta
sending cofibrations to weak equivalences.
By
theorem

3.43
, we know that semisimplicial sets form a cofibration category, so we may apply the glueing lemma in the standard way to reduce the claim to Leibniz applications of
\eta
to boundary inclusions.
By induction on dimension and the usual uses of 2-out-of-3 and closure of cofibrations that are weak equivalences under pushout, the claim reduces further to
\eta_{\Delta^{n}}\colon\Delta^{n}\to UL\Delta^{n}
being a weak equivalence for
[n]\in\Delta_{+}
.
Recall that both
L
and
U
are monoidal with respect to the join and that weak equivalences are closed under join (
corollary

3.50
),
Since
\Delta^{n}
is the
(n+1)
-fold join of
\top
, it thus suffices to check that
\eta_{\top}\colon\top\to UL\top
is a weak equivalence.
This is the statement of
corollary

3.64
.
∎

Remark 3.66
.

In a classical setting,
lemma

3.65
is usually proved by first showing that
\epsilon
is valued in weak equivalences in simplicial sets and then appealing to a triangle law of the adjunction
L\dashv U
and the fact that
L
reflects weak equivalences (which is often taken to be the definition of weak equivalences in semisimplicial sets).
However, in order to be able to use this strategy, we would need to know that
L
reflects weak equivalences, which will be proven (for a certain definition of weak equivalences in simplicial sets) precisely using
lemma

3.65
.
∎

Remark 3.67
.

Let a
horn extension
be a pushout of a horn inclusion and a
simultaneous horn extension
be a pushout of a coproduct of horn inclusions.
It is possible to continue differently in the second half of the proof of
lemma

3.65
and directly exhibit the Leibniz application of
\eta
to boundary inclusions as
\omega
-composites of pushout joins of maps of the form in
lemma

3.62
with boundary inclusions (allowing suitably augmented the case of dimension
-1
) on both sides.
After unfolding various pushout joins and the induction of
lemma

3.62
, this shows that for any semisimplicial set
A
there are
\omega
-composites of simultaneous horn extensions
f
and
g
such that
g=f\eta_{A}
, i.e.
ULA
can be obtained from
A
by first countably infinitely many times filling systems of independent horns and then countably infinitely many times removing fillers for systems of independent horns.
∎

Corollary 3.68
.

The functor
UL
creates weak equivalences.

Proof.

By
lemma

3.65
and 2-out-of-3.
∎

Corollary 3.69
.

U
sends trivial cofibrations to cofibrations that are weak equivalences.

Proof.

By closure properties
corollaries

3.40
and
3.42
of cofibrations that are weak equivalences, it suffices to show that
U
sends horn inclusions to weak equivalences, i.e. that
UL
sends generating anodyne maps to weak equivalences.
This holds by
corollary

3.68
.
∎

Lemma 3.70
.

The functor
U
sends trivial fibrations to weak equivalences.

Proof.

Let
Y\to X
be a trivial fibration in simplicial sets.
By adjointness,
UY\to UX
is a map lifting against cofibrations in semisimplicial sets.
If
UX
was fibrant, we could use
corollary

3.17
to show that
UY\to UX
is a homotopy equivalence.
Note that the only use of fibrancy of
UX
in the proof of that statement is to produce a constant loop
I\otimes UX\to UX
on
\overline{\operatorname{id}_{UX}}\in\hom(UX,UX)_{0}
.
However, we can use the simplicial structure to produce such a constant loop via

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 20.19507pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.19507pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.88553pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 302.45105pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{UX\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\qed

Note that the above proof does not make use of cofibrancy of
UX
in simplicial sets, which we cannot have constructively.

Define now the
weak equivalences
in simplicial sets as the closure of trivial cofibrations and trivial fibrations under 2-out-of-6.
Classically, note that (cofibration, weak equivalence, fibration) is the usual Kan model structure: the cofibrations and trivial cofibrations are defined as usual and that is enough to determine the model structure.

Corollary 3.71
.

The functor
U
preserves weak equivalences.

Proof.

By
corollaries

3.69
,
3.70
and
3.22
.
∎

Corollary 3.72
.

The functor
L
creates weak equivalences.

Proof.

For preservation, recall weak equivalences in semisimplicial sets by definition lie in the closure of anodyne maps under 2-out-of-6 and that
L
sends anodyne maps to trivial cofibrations.
Reflection is
corollary

3.71
and reflection in
corollary

3.68
.
∎

Finally, we have related our definition of weak equivalence in semisimplicial sets with the one of the classical development.
This allows us to conclude as follows.

Theorem 3.73

(Classical logic)
.

In the triple of functors

\textstyle{\widehat{\Delta_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\bot}
\scriptstyle{\bot}
\textstyle{\widehat{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}

relating semisimplicial sets and simplicial sets where
U
is the forgetful functor:

•

the maps
L
and
U
are weak equivalences of cofibration categories,

•

the maps
U
and
R
, restricted to fibrant objects, are weak equivalences of fibration categories,

•

both adjunctions descend to equivalences of homotopy categories.

Proof.

As left adjoints,
L
and
U
preserve all colimits.
We have already noted that
L
and
U
preserve cofibration; they preserve weak equivalences by
corollaries

3.71
and
3.72
.
Thus, they are exact functors of cofibration categories.
By adjointness,
U
and
R
become exact functors of fibration categories.

As a consequence, both adjunctions descend to adjunctions of homotopy categories (which for the case of the right adjoints are constructed equivalently using only the fibrant objects).
Recall that weak equivalences between fibration categories can be tested at the level of homotopy categories.
Note that
U
as an exact functor between cofibration categories is a weak equivalence exactly if it is a weak equivalence as an exact functor between fibration categories, as the weak equivalence coincide.
As claims will thus follow if we can verify that
U
descends to an equivalence on homotopy categories.

By
lemma

3.65
, the unit
\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}\to UL
is valued in weak equivalences.
Using classically logic, we can also show that the counit
\epsilon\colon LU\to\operatorname{Id}
is valued in weak equivalences: since every simplicial set is cofibrant, the claim reduces as usual to
\epsilon_{\Delta^{n}}
being weak equivalence for
n\geq 0
.
It can be seen directly that its source and target are contractible (see for instance
[
KS17
, Lemma 6.2]
), but we can also deduce it in a way that will generalize to the marked case using 2-out-of-3 in the triangle identity
\epsilon L\circ L\eta=\operatorname{id}
since
L
preserves weak equivalences by
corollary

4.58
,
From the above facts about
\eta
and
\epsilon
, it follows that the adjunction
L\dashv U
descends to an equivalence between homotopy categories.
∎

One might wonder when a semisimplicial set can be endowed with degeneracy operators, i.e. lies in the essential image of
U
.
As proven in
[
RS71
, Lemma 5.6]
, the free simplicial set adjunction is monadic.
(As the author learned from Paolo Capriotti, using classical logic, it is also comonadic.)
So to endow a semisimplicial set with degeneracy operators is to find an algebra structure for it with respect to the monad
UL
.

As shown geometrically in
[
RS71
]
, every fibrant semisimplicial set admits degeneracy operators.
A purely combinatorial proof of this statement is
[
McC13
]
; however, the proof method is non-constructive, namely by choosing degeneracies at every level only for those simplices which do not lie in the image of certain degeneracy operators already constructed at lower levels (the images of the constructed degeneracy operators are not decidable subsets).
It seems unlikely that the statement holds constructively.

Given a fibrant semisimplicial set
A
, note that we can find an algebra structure with respect to
UL
as a pointed endofunctor, i.e. a retraction
r\colon ULX\to X
to
\eta_{X}
, as in the proof of the following lemma.
This corresponds to a system of degeneracy operators
s
that are not required to satisfy the simplicial identities of the form
ss=s
.
For a direct constructive of such pseudo-degeneracy operators, see
[
BCH15
, Proposition 7.10]
.

Lemma 3.74
.

For fibrant
X
, the map
\eta_{X}\colon X\to ULX
is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

Construct a retraction
r
to
\eta_{X}
by taking a lift in the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{ULX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}

using
lemmas

3.65
and
3.26
.
Then note that

\displaystyle\eta_{X}\circ r

\displaystyle=ULr\circ\eta_{ULX}

\displaystyle\sim ULr\circ UL\eta_{X}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{id}_{ULX}

where the homotopy is due to
lemma

3.56
.
∎

We have a unique map
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}
natural in semicategories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
and compatible with associativity and symmetry of the two symmetric monoidal structures.
This induces also a map
A\times B\to A\otimes B
natural in semisimplicial sets
A
and
B
with corresponding properties.

The following statement is usually proved by choosing degeneracies as explained earlier.
As this does not work in a constructive setting, we are forced to come up with a different proof.

Theorem 3.75
.

Given fibrant semisimplicial sets
X
and
Y
, the canonical comparison map

X\times Y\to X\otimes Y

between the cartesian and the geometric product is a weak equivalence.

Proof.

Note that
\eta_{X\otimes Y}\colon X\otimes Y\to UL(X\otimes Y)
is a weak equivalence by
lemma

3.65
.
Postcomposing with it, it will suffice by 2-out-of-3 to show that
X\times Y\to UL(X\otimes Y)
is a weak equivalence.
Note that
UL(X\otimes Y)\simeq U(LX\times LY)\simeq ULX\times ULY
.
Under this isomorphism, the map in question corresponds to the product
\eta_{X}\times\eta_{Y}
.
Since
\eta_{X}
and
\eta_{Y}
are homotopy equivalences by
lemma

3.74
, so is their product by
corollary

3.30
.
∎

Note that closure under product fails to hold for weak equivalence.
This explains why the combinatorial complexity of
lemma

3.56
is necessary in the above proof.

3.9.
Subdivision

Let us write
L\dashv U
for both the free category on a semicategory and the free simplicial set on a semisimplicial set adjunction.

3.9.1.
Simplicial subdivision

We recall the definition of the subdivision functor
\operatorname{Sd}
on simplicial sets.
Let
T
be the full subcategory of
[[1],\Delta]
restricted to maps in
\Delta_{+}
.
Note that
\operatorname{ev}_{1}\colon T\to\Delta
is a Grothendieck opfibration: the cobase change of
[a]\to[m]
along
[m]\to[n]
returns the right factor of the Reedy factorization of the composite
[a]\to[n]
.
Under the inverse of the Grothendieck construction, this induces a functor
P\colon\Delta\to\mathbf{Cat}
.
Now
\operatorname{Sd}
is defined as the composition of the cocontinuous extension of
P
with
N
(observe that this coincides with the cocontinuous extension of
NP
).
Note that the subdivision functor extends canonically to augmented simplicial sets.

3.9.2.
Semisimplicial subdivision

We also have a subdivision functor
\operatorname{Sd}
on semisimplicial sets.
Recall that
\Delta_{+}
as a category is freely generated by a semicategory.
Seeing semisimplicial sets as semicategorical presheaves over that semicategory,
\operatorname{Sd}
is defined as the composite of the semicategory of elements functor
{\textstyle{\int}\vphantom{\int}_{\hskip-1.63885pt+}}
followed by the semisimplicial nerve
N
.
It is cocontinuous, and its restriction
\operatorname{sd}
to representables is described by the top row in the following diagram commuting up to natural isomorphism:

\textstyle{\Delta_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Delta_{+})_{/-}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{SemiCat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{\widehat{\Delta_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Cat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N}
\textstyle{\widehat{\Delta}\hbox to0.0pt{;\hss}}

here, the top left arrow denotes the categorical slice of the semicategory generating
\Delta_{+}
and the middle and right vertical arrows are the free category and free simplicial set functors, respectively.
This shows that the free simplicial set functor commutes with subdivision.
Note that the subdivision functor extends canonically to augmented semisimplicial sets.

Semisimplicial subdivision is simpler than simplicial subdivision.
Given a simplicial set
A
with decidable degeneracies, we can compute

(\operatorname{Sd}A)_{n}\simeq\coprod_{k\geq 0,x\in A^{\mathsf{nd}}_{k}}\{\sigma\in N((\Delta_{+})_{/[k]})_{n}\mathrel{|}\sigma(n)=([k],\operatorname{id}_{[k]})\}

where
A^{\mathsf{nd}}_{k}
denotes the set of non-degenerate
k
-simplices of
A
.
For a general simplicial set
A
, we do not have a description of
(\operatorname{Sd}A)_{n}
without quotients.
In contrast, the set of
n
-simplices of the subdivision of a semisimplicial set is describable without quotients in general.

3.9.3.
Last and first vertex maps

Recall the inclusion
i\colon\Delta\to\mathbf{Cat}
.
We have a natural transformation
\Delta_{/-}\to i
that on
[n]
is the functor
\Delta_{/[n]}\to[n]
sending
f\colon[k]\to[n]
to
f(k)
.
Extending cocontinuously, we get a map
\int\to Q
where
Q
is the fundamental category functor, left adjoint to the simplicial nerve
N
.
Equivalently, this is a map
\int N\to\operatorname{Id}
between endofunctors on categories, known as the
categorical last vertex map
.
Dually, we have the
categorical first vertex map

\int N\to(-)^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Confusingly, we also have a map
N\int\to\operatorname{Id}
, given on representables by
N
applied to the map
\Delta_{/-}\to i
.
Note that we have a map
P\to\Delta_{/-}
, inducing a map
\operatorname{Sd}\to N\int
.
We thus obtain the
simplicial last vertex map

\operatorname{Sd}\to\operatorname{Id}
.
Dually, we also have a
simplicial first vertex map

\operatorname{Sd}\to(-)^{\operatorname{op}}
where
(-)^{\operatorname{op}}
denotes the opposite simplicial set functor, precomposition with the opposite functor restricted to
\Delta
.

These maps do not have semicategorical or semisimplicial analogues.
Recall that the forgetful functors commute with the nerves, i.e.
NU\simeq UN
, and that the induced Beck-Chevalley map
LN\to NL
is an isomorphism.
We have a canonical map
\int_{+}U\to U\int
, by adjointness giving rise to a map
L\int_{+}\to\int L
.
Combining these with the categorical last vertex map, we obtain the
approximation to a semicategorical last vertex map

\textstyle{{\textstyle\int}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{L{\textstyle{\int}\vphantom{\int}_{\hskip-1.63885pt+}}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\textstyle\int}LN\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\textstyle\int}NL\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Dually, we have the
approximation to a semicategorical first vertex map

{\textstyle\int}N\to(-)^{\operatorname{op}}L
.

3.9.4.
Indexed join

Let
(\mathcal{C},\top,\otimes)
be a monoidal category.
Recall the Day convolution monoidal structure induced on presheaves over
\mathcal{C}
.
Given presheaves
A,B
over
\mathcal{C}
and a presheaf
F
over
\int A\times\int B
, the
indexed Day convolution

A\otimes_{F}B
is the left Kan extension of
F
along

\textstyle{\int A\times\int B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Let
A\boxtimes B
denote the external product of
A
and
B
.
Note that we have an equivalence

\widehat{{\textstyle\int}A\times{\textstyle\int}B}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}}/(A\boxtimes B).

With respect to this equivalence, left Kan extension along the first arrow in the previous diagram is the forgetful functor.
Thus we recover the usual Day convolution
A\otimes B
if
F
is terminal.

As a special case of the above construction, we obtain the
indexed join
on augmented simplicial sets.
This transfers to the indexed join on simplicial sets via terminal augmentation (right Kan extension).
Explicitly, given simplicial sets
A,B
and a presheaf
F
over
\int A\times\int B
, an
n
-simplex of
A\star_{F}B
is either an
n
-simplex of
A
or
B
or a decomposition
[n]=[a]\star[b]
with
x\in A_{a},y\in B_{b}
and
t\in F(([a],x),([b],y))
.
Note that we recover
A\star B
if
F
is terminal and
A+B
if
F
is initial.
Mirroring the notation for the join, we write

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\textstyle{A\star_{F}B}

for the induced Reedy cofibrant span of inclusions.

Write
X_{\mathsf{t}}
for the terminal augmentation of a semisimplicial set
X
.
Fixing simplicial sets
A
and
B
, note that right Kan extension along
\int A\times\int B\to\int A_{\mathsf{t}}\times\int B_{\mathsf{t}}
is parametric left adjoint (recall that a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
where
\mathcal{C}
has an initial object is called parametric left adjoint if the induced functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}_{\backslash F0}
is left adjoint).
Therefore, the functor sending a presheaf
F
over
\int A\times\int B
to
A\star_{F}B
is also parametric left adjoint, i.e. the induced functor

\textstyle{\widehat{\int A\times\int B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\Delta}_{\backslash A+B}}

is left adjoint.
Its right adjoint sends a cospan of
m_{0}\colon A\to Z
and
m_{1}\colon B\to Z
to the presheaf over
\int A\times\int B
sending
x\in A_{a}
and
y\in B_{b}
to the set of
z\in Z_{a\star b}
such that
z[\iota_{0}]=m_{0}(x)
and
z[\iota_{1}]=m_{1}(y)
.

Fix now only the simplicial set
A
and consider the functor sending a simplicial set
B
and a presheaf
F
over
\int A\times\int B
to the inclusion
\iota_{0}\colon A\to A\star_{F}B
.
Note that the input data can equivalently be described as presheaves over
\int A_{\mathsf{t}}\times\Delta
, i.e. presheaves over
\int A_{\mathsf{t}}
valued in simplicial sets.
Similar to the above, this functor has a right.
Explicitly, it sends
m_{0}\colon A\to Z
to the presheaf over
\int A_{\mathsf{t}}
valued in simplicial sets sending
[-1]
to
Z
and
x\in A_{a}
to
Z_{\backslash x}
.
A dual discussion applies when fixing the simplicial set
B
.

We can also consider the indexed join as a functor to simplicial sets from the category of triples
(A,B,F)
of simplicial sets
A,B
and a presheaf
F
over
\int A\times\int B
.
A morphism
(A_{0},B_{0},F_{0})\to(A_{1},B_{1},F_{1})
in the latter category consists of maps
f\colon A_{0}\to A_{1}
and
g\colon B_{0}\to B_{1}
with a natural transformation
F_{0}\to F_{1}\circ(\int f\times\int g)
.
Then we have a right adjoint to the indexed join sending a simplicial set
Z
to the triple
(Z,Z,F)
where
F((a,x),(b,y))
is the set of maps
[a]\star[b]\to Z
that restrict to
x
and
y
on
\iota_{0}
and
\iota_{1}
, respectively.

The above discussion of indexed joins applies just as well to semisimplicial sets.
We use the same notation and terminology there.

3.9.5.
Relating
\operatorname{Sd}A
and
A

Given a simplicial set
A
, we have a comparison map between
\operatorname{Sd}A
and
A
given by the last vertex map above.
Using classical logic, one may show that this map is a weak equivalence.
Given a semisimplicial set
A
, we do not have such a comparison map between
\operatorname{Sd}A
and
A
.
However, we can still relate them via a cospan

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{A}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Sd}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{ULA}

of weak equivalences.
Here, the left leg is a cofibration and weak equivalence by
lemma

3.65
.
The right leg is the composite

\textstyle{\operatorname{Sd}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{\operatorname{Sd}A}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{UL\operatorname{Sd}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{ULA}

where
m
is
U
applied to the simplicial last vertex map on
LA
.
Note that
m
is a homotopy equivalence on representables: as is well-known, the last vertex map
\Delta_{+}/[n]\to[n]
is a homotopy equivalence in categories with respect to the interval
[1]
, and the simplicial nerve as well as
U
(by
corollary

3.55
) preserve homotopy equivalences.
By cocontinuity and standard uses of the glueing lemma, it then follows that it is a weak equivalence for all
A
.

The above cospan has the drawback that
ULA
is infinite even if
A
is finite.
The goal of this subsubsection is to construct an alternative cospan of weak equivalences relating
A
and
\operatorname{Sd}A
that will be Reedy cofibrant, have anodyne legs, and have a finite summit if
A
is finite.

Fix a semisimplicial set
A
.
Although we lack a map
\operatorname{Sd}A\to A
, we still have this map after applying categories of elements:

\textstyle{{\textstyle\int}\operatorname{Sd}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{{\textstyle\int}N{\textstyle{\int}\vphantom{\int}_{\hskip-1.63885pt+}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L{\textstyle{\int}\vphantom{\int}_{\hskip-1.63885pt+}}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\textstyle\int}A\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Here, the middle map is the semicategorical approximation to the last vertex map.
Dually, we obtain a map
{\textstyle\int}\operatorname{Sd}A\to({\textstyle\int}A)^{\operatorname{op}}
using the semicategorical approximation to the first vertex map.
Taking the composite

\textstyle{F(A)\colon({\textstyle\int}A\times{\textstyle\int}\operatorname{Sd}A)^{\operatorname{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\textstyle\int}A)^{\operatorname{op}}\times{\textstyle\int}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hom}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

gives a Reedy cofibrant span

(3.22)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-1.42038pt\raise-19.36008pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.47511pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 19.88396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 39.01791pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Sd}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.10373pt\raise-20.24998pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.61594pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-6.23866pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\star_{F(A)}\operatorname{Sd}A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

functorial and cocontinuous in
A
.
For a monomorphism
f\colon A\to B
, note that the induced morphism from
F(A)
to the restriction of
F(B)
along the sieves
\int A\to\int B
and
\int\operatorname{Sd}A\to\int\operatorname{Sd}B
is an isomorphism.

Consider now the legs of (
3.22
) as natural transformations in
A
.

Lemma 3.76
.

Pushout application of the left leg of (
3.22
) sends cofibrations to anodyne maps.

Proof.

By cocontinuity, it will suffice to show that the pushout application to
\partial\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{n}
is anodyne.
This is the pushout corner map in the following square:

(3.23)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 13.37617pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.37617pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\partial\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.37617pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.11111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 37.37617pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\partial\Delta^{n}\star_{F(\partial\Delta^{n})}\operatorname{Sd}\partial\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 84.18793pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-9.88834pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.16994pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.16994pt\raise-31.44443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\star_{F(\Delta^{n})}\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Given subobjects
u\colon U\to\Delta^{n}
and
v\colon V\to\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}
, let us abbreviate
U\star^{\prime}V
the indexed join of
U
and
V
with respect to the restriction of
F
along
\int u
and
\int\operatorname{Sd}v
.
The rest of the argument proceeds by working with subobjects of
\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}
.
The square (
3.23
) isomorphically writes as

\textstyle{\partial\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\operatorname{Sd}\partial\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

Note that the pushout in this square is
\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\operatorname{Sd}\partial\Delta^{n}
, so the pushout corner map in (
3.23
) is

\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\operatorname{Sd}\partial\Delta^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

We decompose the cofibration
\operatorname{Sd}\partial\Delta^{n}\to\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}
into a finite composite of pushouts of boundary inclusions.
Note that all attaching maps
f\colon\Delta^{m}\to\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}
of this relative cell complex are monomorphisms.
By cocontinuity of the indexed join with fixed first argument, it will suffice to show that

(3.24)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.2437pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.2437pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\partial\Delta^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.2437pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.2437pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Delta^{n}\star^{\prime}\Delta^{m}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

is anodyne for all such attaching maps
f
.
Recall that
f\colon\Delta^{m}\to\operatorname{Sd}\Delta^{n}
means a map
f\colon[m]\to(\Delta_{+})_{/[n]}
.
Denote the first vertex as
f(0)\colon[k]\to[n]
.
The map (
3.24
) is a pushout of

(3.25)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.69041pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.69041pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Delta^{k}\star\partial\Delta^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.69041pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.69041pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Delta^{k}\star\Delta^{m}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

which is anodyne by iterated applications of

.
∎

.
